<span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Government_of_Australia" title="Government of Australia"><img alt="Government of Australia" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/40px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="31" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/60px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg/80px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Australia.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="397" /></a></span></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar" style="text-align:center;"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Ben_Chifley_sidebar" title="Template:Ben Chifley sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Ben_Chifley_sidebar" title="Template talk:Ben Chifley sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Ben_Chifley_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Joseph Benedict Chifley</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="/tʃ/: &#39;ch&#39; in &#39;China&#39;">tʃ</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;f&#39; in &#39;find&#39;">f</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span></span>/</a></span></span>;<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> 22 September 1885 – 13 June 1951) was an Australian politician who served as the 16th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a>, from 1945 to 1949, holding office as the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Australian Labor Party</a> (ALP).
</p><p>Chifley was born in <a href="/wiki/Bathurst,_New_South_Wales" title="Bathurst, New South Wales">Bathurst, New South Wales</a>, and joined the <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales_Government_Railways" title="New South Wales Government Railways">New South Wales Government Railways</a> after leaving school, eventually qualifying as an <a href="/wiki/Engine_driver" class="mw-redirect" title="Engine driver">engine driver</a>. He was prominent in the <a href="/wiki/Trade_union_movement" class="mw-redirect" title="Trade union movement">trade union movement</a> before entering politics, and was also a director of <i><a href="/wiki/The_National_Advocate" title="The National Advocate">The National Advocate</a></i>. After several previous unsuccessful candidacies, Chifley was elected to parliament in the <a href="/wiki/1928_Australian_federal_election" title="1928 Australian federal election">1928 federal election</a>. In 1931, he was appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Defence_(Australia)" title="Minister for Defence (Australia)">Minister for Defence</a> in the government of <a href="/wiki/James_Scullin" title="James Scullin">James Scullin</a>. He served in cabinet for less than a year before losing his seat at the <a href="/wiki/1931_Australian_federal_election" title="1931 Australian federal election">1931 federal election</a>, which saw the government suffer an <a href="/wiki/Electoral_wipeout" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral wipeout">electoral wipeout</a>.
</p><p>After his electoral defeat, Chifley remained involved in politics as a party official, siding with the federal Labor leadership against the <a href="/wiki/Lang_Labor" title="Lang Labor">Lang Labor</a> faction. He served on a <a href="/wiki/Royal_commission" title="Royal commission">royal commission</a> into the banking system in 1935, and in 1940 became a senior public servant in the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Munitions" title="Department of Munitions">Department of Munitions</a>. Chifley was re-elected to parliament later that year, on his third attempt since 1931. He was appointed <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">Treasurer</a> in the new <a href="/wiki/Curtin_government" title="Curtin government">Curtin government</a> in 1941, as one of the few Labor MPs with previous ministerial experience. The following year Chifley was additionally made <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Postwar_Reconstruction" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Postwar Reconstruction">Minister for Postwar Reconstruction</a>, making him one of the most powerful members of the government. He became prime minister following Curtin's death in office in 1945, defeating caretaker prime minister <a href="/wiki/Frank_Forde" title="Frank Forde">Frank Forde</a> in <a href="/wiki/1945_Australian_Labor_Party_leadership_election" title="1945 Australian Labor Party leadership election">a leadership ballot</a>.
</p><p>At the <a href="/wiki/1946_Australian_federal_election" title="1946 Australian federal election">1946 Australian federal election</a>, Chifley was re-elected with a slightly reduced majority – the first time that an incumbent Labor government had won re-election. The war <a href="/wiki/Victory_over_Japan_Day" title="Victory over Japan Day">had ended</a> a month after he took office, and over the following three years his government embarked on an ambitious program of social reforms and nation-building schemes. These included the expansion of the <a href="/wiki/Welfare_state" title="Welfare state">welfare state</a>, increased the <a href="/wiki/Post-war_immigration_to_Australia" title="Post-war immigration to Australia">Post-war immigration to Australia</a>, and the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_University" title="Australian National University">Australian National University</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Security_Intelligence_Organisation" title="Australian Security Intelligence Organisation">Australian Security Intelligence Organisation</a> (ASIO), and the <a href="/wiki/Snowy_Mountains_Scheme" title="Snowy Mountains Scheme">Snowy Mountains Scheme</a>. Some of the new legislation was successfully challenged in the <a href="/wiki/High_Court_of_Australia" title="High Court of Australia">High Court</a>, and as a result <a href="/wiki/Australian_referendum,_1946_(Social_Services)" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian referendum, 1946 (Social Services)">the constitution was amended</a> to give the federal government extended powers over social services.
</p><p>Some of Chifley's more interventionist economic policies were poorly received by Australian business, particularly an attempt to nationalise banks. His government was defeated at the <a href="/wiki/1949_Australian_federal_election" title="1949 Australian federal election">1949 Australian federal election</a>, which brought <a href="/wiki/Robert_Menzies" title="Robert Menzies">Robert Menzies</a>' <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party</a> to power for the first time. He stayed on as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Australia)">Leader of the Opposition</a> until his death, which came a few months after the <a href="/wiki/1951_Australian_federal_election" title="1951 Australian federal election">1951 Australian federal election</a>; Labor did not return to government until 1972. For his contributions to post-war prosperity, Chifley is often regarded as one of Australia's greatest prime ministers. He is held in particularly high regard by the Labor Party, with his "<a href="/wiki/Light_on_the_hill" class="mw-redirect" title="Light on the hill">light on the hill</a>" speech seen as seminal in both the history of the party and the broader <a href="/wiki/Australian_labour_movement" title="Australian labour movement">Australian labour movement</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Chifley was born at 29 Havannah Street, <a href="/wiki/Bathurst,_New_South_Wales" title="Bathurst, New South Wales">Bathurst, New South Wales</a>, on 22 September 1885. He was the first of three sons born to Roman Catholic parents: Mary Anne (née Corrigan) and Patrick Chifley II. His father, a blacksmith, was born in Bathurst to Irish immigrants from <a href="/wiki/County_Tipperary" title="County Tipperary">County Tipperary</a>, while his mother was born in <a href="/wiki/County_Fermanagh" title="County Fermanagh">County Fermanagh</a>, in present-day <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland" title="Northern Ireland">Northern Ireland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp19612_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp19612-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the age of five, Chifley was sent to live with his widowed grandfather, Patrick Chifley I, who had a small farm at <a href="/wiki/Limekilns,_New_South_Wales" title="Limekilns, New South Wales">Limekilns, New South Wales</a>, while his aunt, Mary Bridget Chifley, kept house for them. Chifley began his education at the local state school, which was known as a "half-time school" due to it being too small to offer daily classes; it shared a single teacher with a neighbouring community.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp19613_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp19613-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> He moved back to his parents' home at the age of 13, following his grandfather's death in January 1899, and attended a <a href="/wiki/Patrician_Brothers" title="Patrician Brothers">Patrician Brothers</a> school for about two years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp19615_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp19615-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> He was a voracious reader from a young age, and would later supplement his limited formal education by attending classes at night schools or <a href="/wiki/Mechanics%27_institutes" class="mw-redirect" title="Mechanics&#39; institutes">mechanics' institutes</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp19616_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp19616-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After leaving school, Chifley's first job was as a cashier's assistant at a local department store. He later worked at a tannery for a period, and then in September 1903 joined the New South Wales Government Railways as a "shop boy" at the Bathurst <a href="/wiki/Locomotive_shed" class="mw-redirect" title="Locomotive shed">locomotive shed</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp19617_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp19617-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Over the following decade, he was promoted through the ranks to engine-cleaner and <a href="/wiki/Fireman_(steam_engine)" title="Fireman (steam engine)">fireman</a>, and then finally in March 1914 to engine-driver.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196111_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196111-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> The position of driver was considered relatively prestigious, and Chifley had to sit various examinations before being certified. He developed an intimate technical understanding of his locomotives, and became a lecturer and instructor at the Bathurst Railway Institute.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196112_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196112-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Chifley drove both goods trains and passenger trains. He was based in Bathurst and worked on the <a href="/wiki/Main_Western_railway_line,_New_South_Wales" title="Main Western railway line, New South Wales">Main Western railway</a>, except for a few months in 1914 when he drove on the <a href="/wiki/Main_Southern_railway_line,_New_South_Wales" title="Main Southern railway line, New South Wales">Main Southern railway</a> and worked out of <a href="/wiki/Harden,_New_South_Wales" title="Harden, New South Wales">Harden, New South Wales</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196111_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196111-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chifley became involved with the <a href="/wiki/Australian_labour_movement" title="Australian labour movement">labour movement</a> as a member of the Locomotive Enginemen's Association.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> He never held executive office, preferring to work as an <a href="/wiki/Union_organizer" title="Union organizer">organiser</a>, but did serve as a divisional delegate to state and federal conferences. He developed a reputation for compromise, maintaining good relations with both the railway management and the more militant sections of the union.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196113_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196113-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> However, Chifley was one of the local leaders of the <a href="/wiki/1917_Australian_general_strike" title="1917 Australian general strike">1917 Australian general strike</a>, and as a result was dismissed from the railway. He and most of the other strikers were eventually reinstated, but lost seniority and related privileges; Chifley was demoted from engine-driver to fireman.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196123_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196123-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Despite repeated lobbying, their pre-1917 benefits were not restored until 1925.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196128_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196128-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> After the strike, the state government of <a href="/wiki/William_Holman" title="William Holman">William Holman</a> also de-registered their union, placing it at a severe disadvantage against <a href="/wiki/Railway_trade_unions_in_Australia" title="Railway trade unions in Australia">other railway unions</a>. Chifley worked to secure its re-registration, which occurred in 1921, and was also involved in the formation of a national union – the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Federated_Union_of_Locomotive_Enginemen" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Federated Union of Locomotive Enginemen">Australian Federated Union of Locomotive Enginemen</a> – in 1920.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196126_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196126-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> He appeared as an expert witness before the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Court_of_Conciliation_and_Arbitration" title="Commonwealth Court of Conciliation and Arbitration">Commonwealth Court of Conciliation and Arbitration</a> in 1924, which subsequently implemented a new federal <a href="/wiki/Industrial_award" title="Industrial award">industrial award</a> for the enginemen.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196127_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196127-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_involvement">Early political involvement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early political involvement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Chifley joined the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Australian Labor Party</a> at a young age, and was involved in state and federal election campaigns as an organiser.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196129_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196129-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> In 1921, he replaced his father on the board of <i><a href="/wiki/The_National_Advocate" title="The National Advocate">The National Advocate</a></i>, a local newspaper that functioned as the mouthpiece of the labour movement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001171_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001171-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> In 1922 and 1924, Chifley unsuccessfully contested Labor <a href="/wiki/Preselection" title="Preselection">preselection</a> for the NSW <a href="/wiki/Electoral_district_of_Bathurst" title="Electoral district of Bathurst">Electoral district of Bathurst</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196130_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196130-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He was eventually chosen as the Labor candidate for the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Macquarie" title="Division of Macquarie">Division of Macquarie</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1925_Australian_federal_election" title="1925 Australian federal election">1925 Australian federal election</a>. Macquarie was a large and diverse electorate, covering an area from Bathurst east across the <a href="/wiki/Blue_Mountains_(New_South_Wales)" title="Blue Mountains (New South Wales)">Blue Mountains</a> to <a href="/wiki/Penrith,_New_South_Wales" title="Penrith, New South Wales">Penrith</a>, on the outskirts of Sydney; it included industrial, agricultural, and mining districts in virtually equal measure. It was one of the most <a href="/wiki/Marginal_seat" title="Marginal seat">marginal seats</a> in the country, and had last been won by Labor in 1919.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196131–32_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196131–32-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Lacking name recognition, Chifley lost the election to the incumbent <a href="/wiki/Nationalist_Party_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Nationalist Party of Australia">Nationalist</a> MP, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Manning" title="Arthur Manning">Arthur Manning</a>. However, he reprised his candidacy in 1928, mounting a campaign that focused on the <a href="/wiki/Bruce_government" class="mw-redirect" title="Bruce government">Bruce government</a>'s unpopular labour policies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001228–229_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001228–229-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> He accused the government of endangering the <a href="/wiki/White_Australia_policy" title="White Australia policy">White Australia policy</a> by allowing Southern European migrant workers into the country, claiming it had "allowed so many dagoes and aliens in Australia that today they are all over the country taking work which rightly belongs to all Australians". The Labor Party recorded a 6.2-point swing in Macquarie, with Chifley becoming one of three candidates in New South Wales to win seats from the government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001230–233_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001230–233-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Early political career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_split_of_1931" title="Australian Labor Party split of 1931">Australian Labor Party split of 1931</a></div>
<p>At the <a href="/wiki/1929_Australian_federal_election" title="1929 Australian federal election">1929 election</a>, Chifley was re-elected on a 10.7-point swing as Labor won a landslide victory. <a href="/wiki/James_Scullin" title="James Scullin">James Scullin</a> became the new prime minister, the fourth member of his party to hold the office. As a <a href="/wiki/Backbench" class="mw-redirect" title="Backbench">backbencher</a> with little parliamentary experience, Chifley did not stand for election to the <a href="/wiki/Scullin_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Scullin Ministry">Scullin Ministry</a>, but did join the Public Accounts Committee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196152–53_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196152–53-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> As the <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression_in_Australia" title="Great Depression in Australia">Great Depression</a> worsened, he defended the government's economic response against criticism from two factions within his own party – economic conservatives led by <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Lyons" title="Joseph Lyons">Joseph Lyons</a> and left-wing populists led by <a href="/wiki/Jack_Lang_(Australian_politician)" title="Jack Lang (Australian politician)">Jack Lang</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> His loyalty paid off in March 1931, when the Labor caucus chose him to fill one of the vacancies in cabinet caused by the resignations of Lyons and <a href="/wiki/James_Fenton_(Australian_politician)" class="mw-redirect" title="James Fenton (Australian politician)">James Fenton</a>. Scullin appointed him Minister for Defence, a portfolio that had been disregarded somewhat in the face of more pressing concerns.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001258_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001258-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> There was little appetite for policy development, and Chifley instead concentrated on finding savings in his department that could be redirected to unemployment relief. He opened up unused military camps to the homeless, and also distributed surplus military clothing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001262_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001262-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:BenChifley2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/BenChifley2.jpg/220px-BenChifley2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="300" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/BenChifley2.jpg/330px-BenChifley2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/BenChifley2.jpg/440px-BenChifley2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="656" data-file-height="895" /></a><figcaption>Chifley in the 1930s</figcaption></figure>
<p>Chifley was somewhat reluctant in his support of the <a href="/wiki/Premiers%27_Plan" title="Premiers&#39; Plan">Premiers' Plan</a>, but believed there was no better alternative and felt bound by the principle of <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_solidarity" class="mw-redirect" title="Cabinet solidarity">cabinet solidarity</a>. His endorsement of the plan, which required cuts to wages and pensions, was received poorly in his own constituency. Many in the local labour movement defected to <a href="/wiki/Lang_Labor" title="Lang Labor">Lang Labor</a>, which opposed the plan, and his own union expelled him in August 1931.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196175–78_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196175–78-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Joseph Lyons reportedly offered Chifley the treasurership as an inducement to join the new <a href="/wiki/United_Australia_Party" title="United Australia Party">United Australia Party</a> (UAP); Chifley declined and remained a member of the Labor Party.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> At the <a href="/wiki/1931_Australian_federal_election" title="1931 Australian federal election">1931 election</a>, Chifley suffered a negative swing of 16.2 points in Macquarie, losing his seat to <a href="/wiki/John_Lawson_(Australian_politician)" title="John Lawson (Australian politician)">John Lawson</a>, the UAP candidate, by just 456 votes on the final count. The Labor Party was reduced to 14 seats out of 75 in the House of Representatives, with five other ministers (including Treasurer <a href="/wiki/Ted_Theodore" title="Ted Theodore">Ted Theodore</a>) and future prime minister <a href="/wiki/John_Curtin" title="John Curtin">John Curtin</a> also losing their seats.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrisp196184–85_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisp196184–85-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Wilderness_years">Wilderness years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Wilderness years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During the Great Depression, with no parliamentary salary and no chance of returning to the railway, Chifley survived on his wife's family's money and his part-ownership of the Bathurst newspaper <i><a href="/wiki/The_National_Advocate" title="The National Advocate">The National Advocate</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-before_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-before-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1938, Chifley and most other Labor supporters in Bathurst joined the <a href="/wiki/Industrial_Labor_Party" title="Industrial Labor Party">Industrial Labor Party</a> (ILP), a breakaway organisation formed by <a href="/wiki/Bob_Heffron" title="Bob Heffron">Bob Heffron</a> and dedicated to thwarting the <a href="/wiki/Lang_Labor" title="Lang Labor">Lang Labor</a> faction that controlled the ALP in New South Wales.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001337–338_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001337–338-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> He was a delegate to the party's annual conference in Sydney in April 1939. After a unity conference in August 1939, the ILP members rejoined the ALP and ended Jack Lang's dominance. Chifley was subsequently elected to the ALP state executive.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001339_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001339-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1935 the <a href="/wiki/Lyons_government" title="Lyons government">Lyons government</a> appointed Chifley as a member of the Royal Commission on Banking, a subject on which he had become an expert. He submitted a minority report advocating that the private banks be nationalised. After an unsuccessful effort to win back Macquarie at the <a href="/wiki/1934_Australian_federal_election" title="1934 Australian federal election">1934 Australian federal election</a>, Chifley finally won his seat back at the <a href="/wiki/1940_Australian_federal_election" title="1940 Australian federal election">1940 Australian federal election</a> on a swing of ten percent.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-before_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-before-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Curtin_government">Curtin government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Curtin government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Chifley was appointed <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">Treasurer of Australia</a> when the Labor leader, John Curtin, formed a mid-term Labor government in 1941 following the collapse of the <a href="/wiki/Menzies_Government_(1939%E2%80%9341)" class="mw-redirect" title="Menzies Government (1939–41)">first Menzies government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-before_28-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-before-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Although deputy Labor leader <a href="/wiki/Frank_Forde" title="Frank Forde">Frank Forde</a> was nominally the deputy in the government, Chifley became the minister Curtin most relied on, controlling most domestic policy while Curtin was preoccupied with the <a href="/wiki/Second_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Second World War">Second World War</a>. Of highest importance was war funding, followed by the strong desire to control inflation. In February 1942, he announced the pegging of wages and profits, the introduction of controls on production, trade and consumption to reduce private spending, and the transfer of surplus personal income to savings and war loans. On 15 April 1942, more price controls were introduced. On 23 July, a uniform income tax was attained when the States were defeated in the <a href="/wiki/High_Court_of_Australia" title="High Court of Australia">High Court of Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Australian_Dictionary_of_Biography" title="Australian Dictionary of Biography">Australian Dictionary of Biography</a> claims Chifley proved himself to be his country's greatest treasurer – fiscally responsible, able to transmit the necessity for a reasonable equality of sacrifice, and capable of managing a wartime economy of complexity and difficulty. Financing the war by increased taxation, loans from the Australian public, and central bank credit, he ensured that the nation did not become burdened with overseas debt, as it had been after <a href="/wiki/First_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First World War">First World War</a>. Every budget was accompanied by his strictures on 'vigorous self-denial', labour discipline and restriction of consumer demand with the aim of controlling a huge accumulation of purchasing power.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_minister">Prime minister</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Prime minister">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Chifley_government" title="Chifley government">Chifley government</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ChifleyEvatt.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/ChifleyEvatt.jpg/220px-ChifleyEvatt.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="157" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/ChifleyEvatt.jpg/330px-ChifleyEvatt.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/94/ChifleyEvatt.jpg 2x" data-file-width="425" data-file-height="303" /></a><figcaption>Chifley (middle) and <a href="/wiki/H._V._Evatt" title="H. V. Evatt">Bert Evatt</a> (left) with <a href="/wiki/Clement_Attlee" title="Clement Attlee">Clement Attlee</a> (right) at the Dominion and British Leaders Conference, London, 1946</figcaption></figure><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Norrie_Chifley_Playford.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Norrie_Chifley_Playford.jpg/220px-Norrie_Chifley_Playford.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="156" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Norrie_Chifley_Playford.jpg/330px-Norrie_Chifley_Playford.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Norrie_Chifley_Playford.jpg/440px-Norrie_Chifley_Playford.jpg 2x" data-file-width="487" data-file-height="346" /></a><figcaption>Chifley (left) meets with <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_South_Australia" title="Premier of South Australia">Premier of South Australia</a> <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Playford_IV" title="Thomas Playford IV">Tom Playford</a> (centre) and <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_South_Australia" title="Governor of South Australia">Governor of South Australia</a> <a href="/wiki/Willoughby_Norrie,_1st_Baron_Norrie" title="Willoughby Norrie, 1st Baron Norrie">Sir Willoughby Norrie</a> (right) in 1946</figcaption></figure>
<p>Following the death of Curtin in July 1945, Forde became Prime Minister since he was the ALP deputy leader. In the ensuing leadership ballot, Chifley defeated Forde to replace him as Prime Minister and Curtin as leader of the ALP. Once the war ended in September, normal political life resumed, and Chifley faced <a href="/wiki/Robert_Menzies" title="Robert Menzies">Robert Menzies</a> and his new <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1946_Australian_federal_election" title="1946 Australian federal election">1946 election</a>, which Chifley won with 54 percent of the <a href="/wiki/Two-party-preferred_vote" title="Two-party-preferred vote">two-party-preferred vote</a>. It marked the first time that an incumbent full-term federal Labor government was re-elected. In the post-war years, Chifley maintained wartime economic controls, including the highly unpopular petrol rationing. He did this partly to help Britain in its postwar economic difficulties.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-inoffice_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-inoffice-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Upon becoming prime minister, Chifley continued as Treasurer and remained so for the entirety of his prime ministership. To date, Chifley is the last prime minister to have been his own Treasurer for a period that was not transitionary, as happened in 1972 and 1991 with <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bob_Hawke" title="Bob Hawke">Bob Hawke</a> respectively. 
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Legislative_achievements">Legislative achievements</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Legislative achievements">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+link+.ambox{margin-top:-1px}html body.mediawiki .mw-parser-output .ambox.mbox-small-left{margin:4px 1em 4px <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:What_Wikipedia_is_not" title="Wikipedia:What Wikipedia is not">Wikipedia's inclusion policy</a>.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">February 2018</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this <figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:NAA_A1200_L84254_Ben_Chifley_at_the_launching_of_the_first_mass-produced_Australian_car_1948.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/NAA_A1200_L84254_Ben_Chifley_at_the_launching_of_the_first_mass-produced_Australian_car_1948.jpg/220px-NAA_A1200_L84254_Ben_Chifley_at_the_launching_of_the_first_mass-produced_Australian_car_1948.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="177" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/NAA_A1200_L84254_Ben_Chifley_at_the_launching_of_the_first_mass-produced_Australian_car_1948.jpg/330px-NAA_A1200_L84254_Ben_Chifley_at_the_launching_of_the_first_mass-produced_Australian_car_1948.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/NAA_A1200_L84254_Ben_Chifley_at_the_launching_of_the_first_mass-produced_Australian_car_1948.jpg/440px-NAA_A1200_L84254_Ben_Chifley_at_the_launching_of_the_first_mass-produced_Australian_car_1948.jpg 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="564" /></a><figcaption>Chifley at the launching of the <a href="/wiki/Holden_48-215" title="Holden 48-215">Holden 48-215</a> (Australia's first Made Car) on 29 November 1948</figcaption></figure>
<p>Feeling secure in an unprecedented position, Chifley looked toward the Labor platform objective of <a href="/wiki/Democratic_socialism" title="Democratic socialism">democratic socialism</a>. According to a biographer of Chifley, his government embarked upon greater intervention in "economic and social affairs", with policies directed towards better workplace conditions, full employment, and the "equalisation of wealth, income and opportunity".<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Chifley was successful in steering the economy into peacetime, and undertook a number of social welfare initiatives, as characterised by fairer pensions and unemployment and sickness benefits, the construction of new universities, technical colleges, and 200,000 houses.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-aged1_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-aged1-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The amount of reforms undertaken was such that, between 1946–49, the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Australia" title="Parliament of Australia">Parliament of Australia</a> passed an at-the-time record 299 <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Acts of Parliament">Acts of Parliament</a>, which was well beyond the previous record of 113 Acts by the <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Fisher" title="Andrew Fisher">Second Fisher government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Among other measures, the Chifley government passed legislation to establish <a href="/wiki/Universal_health_care" title="Universal health care">universal health care</a> modelled on the British <a href="/wiki/National_Health_Service" title="National Health Service">National Health Service</a>, including a free formulary of essential medicines.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> This was successfully opposed as unconstitutional in the <a href="/wiki/High_Court_of_Australia" title="High Court of Australia">High Court of Australia</a> by the Australian branches of the <a href="/wiki/British_Medical_Association" title="British Medical Association">British Medical Association</a>, who were the precursors of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Medical_Association" title="Australian Medical Association">Australian Medical Association</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Attorney-General_(Vic)_ex_rel_Dale_v_Commonwealth" title="Attorney-General (Vic) ex rel Dale v Commonwealth"><i>First Pharmaceutical Benefits case</i></a>.<sup id="cite_ref-1st_Pharmaceutical_case_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1st_Pharmaceutical_case-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> One of the few successful <a href="/wiki/Referendums_in_Australia" title="Referendums in Australia">referendums</a> to modify the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Constitution">Australian Constitution</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Australian_referendum,_1946_(Social_Services)" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian referendum, 1946 (Social Services)">1946 Social Services referendum</a>, made possible many of the Chifley government's other legislative initiatives in social welfare and social provision and permitted federal legislation over pharmaceutical benefits and medical and dental services.<sup id="cite_ref-alp_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-alp-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-asio_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-asio-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001443–44_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001443–44-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> It also authorised federal legislation with respect to pensions, benefits, and allowances.<sup id="cite_ref-john.curtin.edu.au_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-john.curtin.edu.au-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Such as, in the same year as the referendum, when concessional rate radio licences were introduced for pensioners, and were later extended to widow pensioners and also to television licences.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> The following year, in 1947, specific racial disqualifications other than those referring to Aboriginal Australians were removed, while the Wife's Allowance became payable to de facto wives who had lived with the pensioner for at least three years.<sup id="cite_ref-aged1_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-aged1-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The subsequent federal legislation in relation to pharmaceutical benefits was deemed constitutional by the High Court.<sup id="cite_ref-2nd_Pharmaceutical_case_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2nd_Pharmaceutical_case-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> This paved the way for the introduction of the <a href="/wiki/Pharmaceutical_Benefits_Scheme" title="Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme">Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme</a> (PBS), an important component of Australia's modern public health system.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From July 1947, a prepayment of Maternity Allowance could be made up to four weeks before the expected date of the birth of the child. Moreover, eligibility for maternity benefits was extended to mothers who were classified as an alien but had lived in Australia for 12 months residence.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Then again that same year, eligibility for Child's Allowance was extended to those wives whose husbands were in <a href="/wiki/Benevolent_Asylum" class="mw-redirect" title="Benevolent Asylum">asylums</a> and to single invalid pensioners who had the custody, care and control of a child. An additional benefit of five shillings per week for the first child became available to a beneficiary who had custody and spent an equivalent or more of the benefit on the child.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Amendments were also made to legislation on Child Endowment to allow Australians temporarily absent from Australia and newly arrived migrants to receive the benefit.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Furthermore, from July 1947, funeral benefits could be paid in respect of claimants for Age Pension or Invalid Pension who would have qualified had they lived.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Under the Social Services Consolidation Act 1947, an additional benefit became payable in cases where a man with one or more dependent children had a female partner, where he was not receiving benefit for his wife; a partial additional benefit became payable for a partially dependent spouse; and wives legally separated or likely to be permanently living apart from their husbands became eligible for benefit.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ben Chifley's Government oversaw the creation of the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Employment_Service" title="Commonwealth Employment Service">Commonwealth Employment Service</a>, the introduction of federal funds to the States for public housing construction and the Acoustic Laboratories Act 1948, which established the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Acoustic_Laboratories" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth Acoustic Laboratories">Commonwealth Acoustic Laboratories</a> to undertake scientific investigations into <a href="/wiki/Hearing_loss" title="Hearing loss">hearing loss</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Federation_Press_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Federation_Press-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Although it failed in its attempts to establish a national health service, the Chifley government was successful in making arrangements with the states to upgrade the quality and availability of hospital treatment. The Mental Institutions Benefits Act 1948 marked the entry of the Commonwealth into mental health funding, where, in return for free treatment, the states were paid a benefit equal to the charges upon the relatives of mental hospital patients.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> The achievements of the Labor governments of Chifley and Curtin in expanding Australia's social welfare services were brought together under the Social Services Consolidation Act 1947, which consolidated the various social services benefits, liberalised some existing social security provisions, and increased the rates of various benefits.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-john.curtin.edu.au_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-john.curtin.edu.au-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-books.google.com_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-books.google.com-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Among the government's other legislative achievements included the establishment of a separate <a href="/wiki/Australian_citizenship" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian citizenship">Australian citizenship</a> in 1948 and the founding of <a href="/wiki/ASIO" class="mw-redirect" title="ASIO">ASIO</a>. Science and education was also expanded, with a reorganisation and enlargement of the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Scientific_and_Industrial_Research_Organisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth Scientific and Industrial Research Organisation">Commonwealth Scientific and Industrial Research Organisation</a> (CSIRO), alongside passing the Australian National University Act which provided post-graduate facilities in Australia and augmented the supply of staff for universities.<sup id="cite_ref-moadoph1_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-moadoph1-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-whitlam_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-whitlam-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Tertiary education extensively benefitted through the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_University" title="Australian National University">Australian National University</a> and the Commonwealth Education Office.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Reconstruction_Training_Scheme" title="Commonwealth Reconstruction Training Scheme">Commonwealth Reconstruction Training Scheme</a> to provide ex-servicemen with the opportunity to undertake a university education, with an interim five-year scholarships established to encourage other able students to attend universities. This was alongside annual grants to universities to provide the necessary staff and accommodation for the influx of assisted students and ex-servicemen.<sup id="cite_ref-whitlam_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-whitlam-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> In addition, returned soldiers were also provided with a war gratuity and entitlement to special unemployment allowances, loans, vocational training, and preference in employment for seven years.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
In July 1948, the Dairy Industry Fund was created with the purpose of stabilising returns from exports, and further financial grants to the States were introduced to assist them in expanding their agricultural activities.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> The establishment of a Coal Industry Tribunal and a Joint Coal Board in 1946 also brought significant gains for miners; and life insurance came to be comprehensively regulated.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:BenChifley3.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/BenChifley3.jpg/170px-BenChifley3.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/28/BenChifley3.jpg/255px-BenChifley3.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/28/BenChifley3.jpg 2x" data-file-width="328" data-file-height="425" /></a><figcaption>Chifley in the 1940s</figcaption></figure>
<p>Among the Chifley government's legislation was the <a href="/wiki/Post-war_immigration_to_Australia#The_Chifley_years" title="Post-war immigration to Australia">post-war immigration scheme</a>, the establishment of <a href="/wiki/Australian_citizenship" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian citizenship">Australian citizenship</a>, the beginning of construction of the <a href="/wiki/Snowy_Mountains_Scheme" title="Snowy Mountains Scheme">Snowy Mountains Scheme</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Cambridge_University_Press_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cambridge_University_Press-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> the establishment and nationalisation of <a href="/wiki/Trans_Australia_Airlines" title="Trans Australia Airlines">Trans Australia Airlines</a> and <a href="/wiki/Qantas" title="Qantas">Qantas</a> respectively, improvements in social services,<sup id="cite_ref-Cambridge_University_Press_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cambridge_University_Press-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> the creation of the Commonwealth Employment Service,<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_32-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> the introduction of federal funds to the States for public housing construction,<sup id="cite_ref-Federation_Press_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Federation_Press-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> the establishment of a Universities Commission for the expansion of university education,<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> and free hospital treatment,<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_32-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> the reorganisation and enlargement of the CSIRO, and the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Rehabilitation_Service" title="Commonwealth Rehabilitation Service">Commonwealth Rehabilitation Service</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-books.google.com_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-books.google.com-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> As noted by one historian, Chifley's government "balanced economic development and welfare support with restraint and regulation and provided the framework for Australia's post-war economic prosperity."<sup id="cite_ref-moadoph1_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-moadoph1-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_controversial_actions">Later controversial actions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Later controversial actions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1947, Chifley announced the government would initiate a <a href="/wiki/Nationalisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Nationalisation">nationalisation</a> of the banks   . This provoked massive opposition from the press, and middle-class opinion turned against Labor. The High Court found Chifley's legislation to be unconstitutional. The government <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_v_Bank_of_New_South_Wales" title="Commonwealth v Bank of New South Wales">appealed the decision in the Privy Council</a>, but it upheld the High Court's decision.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-inoffice_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-inoffice-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, Chifley's government did succeed in passing the Banking Act 1945 and the Commonwealth Bank Act 1945 which gave the government control over monetary policy and established the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Bank_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth Bank of Australia">Commonwealth Bank</a> as Australia's national bank.<sup id="cite_ref-moadoph1_58-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-moadoph1-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/1948_Queensland_railway_strike" title="1948 Queensland railway strike">1948 Queensland railway strike</a>, Chifley barred striking workers from being eligible for unemployment benefits.<sup id="cite_ref-PeoplesChampion_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PeoplesChampion-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>  A prolonged and bitter <a href="/wiki/1949_Australian_coal_strike" title="1949 Australian coal strike">strike in the coal industry</a> began in June 1949 and caused unemployment and hardship. Chifley saw the strike as a move by the <a href="/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Australia" title="Communist Party of Australia">Communist Party</a> to challenge Labor's place as the party of the working class, and he sent in 13,000 army troops to break the strike.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-inoffice_31-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-inoffice-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-PeoplesChampion_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PeoplesChampion-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Early on in the strike, Chifley and <a href="/wiki/H._V._Evatt" title="H. V. Evatt">H. V. Evatt</a> froze <a href="/wiki/Australian_Coal_and_Shale_Employees%27_Federation" title="Australian Coal and Shale Employees&#39; Federation">Miner's Federation</a> funds and "introduced legislation aimed at starving the workers back to work".<sup id="cite_ref-PeoplesChampion_65-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PeoplesChampion-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1949 in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Representatives_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="House of Representatives (Australia)">House of Representatives</a>, Chifley stated that the Labor Party was a "bulwark against communism", and that the most effective way of weakening the strength of the Communist Party was "improving the conditions of the people".<sup id="cite_ref-PeoplesChampion_65-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PeoplesChampion-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Despite this, Menzies exploited the rising <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> hysteria to portray Labor as soft on Communism. These events, together with a perception that Chifley and Labor had grown increasingly arrogant in office, led to the Liberal election victory at the 1949 election. While Labor won an additional four seats in a House of Representatives that had been expanded from 74 seats to 121 seats, Menzies and the Coalition won an additional 48. Labor retained a <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Senate</a> majority however.<sup id="cite_ref-elections_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-elections-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition">Opposition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Opposition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Chifley was now aged 64 and in poor health (like Curtin, he was a lifelong smoker), but he refused to retire from politics. Though out of government, having retained a Senate majority, Chifley continued as Labor leader and became <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Australia)" title="Leader of the Opposition (Australia)">Leader of the Opposition</a>. The opposition Senate majority would frequently ensure the passing of Labor amendments, or outright blocking, of <a href="/wiki/Menzies_Government_(1949%E2%80%9366)" class="mw-redirect" title="Menzies Government (1949–66)">Menzies Government</a> legislation.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-afteroffice_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-afteroffice-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Menzies responded by introducing a bill to ban the Communist Party of Australia in 1950. He expected Chifley to reject it and give him an excuse to call a <a href="/wiki/Double_dissolution" title="Double dissolution">double dissolution</a> election. Menzies apparently hoped to repeat his "soft-on-Communism" theme to win a majority in both chambers.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-afteroffice_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-afteroffice-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-moadoph2_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-moadoph2-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, Chifley let the bill pass after a redraft (it was ultimately <a href="/wiki/Australian_Communist_Party_v_Commonwealth" title="Australian Communist Party v Commonwealth">thrown out</a> by the High Court). However, when Chifley rejected Menzies' Commonwealth Banking Bill a few months later, Menzies called <a href="/wiki/1951_Australian_federal_election" title="1951 Australian federal election">a double dissolution election for April 1951</a>. Although Chifley managed to lead Labor to a five-seat swing in the House, Labor lost six seats in the Senate, giving the Coalition control of both chambers.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-afteroffice_67-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-afteroffice-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-elections_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-elections-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:BenChifely_lyinginstate_1951.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/BenChifely_lyinginstate_1951.jpg/220px-BenChifely_lyinginstate_1951.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/BenChifely_lyinginstate_1951.jpg/330px-BenChifely_lyinginstate_1951.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/BenChifely_lyinginstate_1951.jpg/440px-BenChifely_lyinginstate_1951.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="452" /></a><figcaption>Chifley <a href="/wiki/Lying_in_state" title="Lying in state">lying in state</a> in Old Parliament House, June 1951</figcaption></figure>
<p>A few weeks later on 13 June 1951, Chifley suffered a heart attack in his room at the <a href="/wiki/Hotel_Kurrajong" title="Hotel Kurrajong">Hotel Kurrajong</a> in <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_22-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-afteroffice_67-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-afteroffice-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chifley at first made light of the sudden chest pains and attempted to dissuade his secretary and confidante, Phyllis Donnelly, who was making him a cup of tea, from calling a doctor. As his condition deteriorated, however, Donnelly called Dr. John Holt, who ordered Chifley's immediate removal to hospital. Chifley died in an ambulance on the way to the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canberra_Hospital" title="Royal Canberra Hospital">Canberra Community Hospital</a>. He was pronounced dead at 10:45&#160;pm.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Menzies heard of Chifley's demise while attending a parliamentary <a href="/wiki/Ball_(dance)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ball (dance)">ball</a> at King's Hall in Parliament House to celebrate the 50th Jubilee of Federation (Chifley was invited but had declined to attend). Menzies was deeply distressed and abandoned his normally impassive demeanour to announce in a halting subdued voice:</p><blockquote><p>It is my very sorrowful duty during this celebration tonight to tell you that Mr Chifley has died. I don't want to try to talk about him now because, although we were political opponents, he was a friend of mine and yours, and a fine Australian. You will all agree that in the circumstances the festivities should end. It doesn't matter about party politics on an occasion such as this. Oddly enough, in Parliament we get on very well. We sometimes find we have the warmest friendships among people whose politics are not ours. Mr Chifley served this country magnificently for years.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Chifley was buried at the Bathurst cemetery on 18 June 1951.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Chifley_House,_Bathurst.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Chifley_House%2C_Bathurst.jpg/220px-Chifley_House%2C_Bathurst.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="192" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Chifley_House%2C_Bathurst.jpg/330px-Chifley_House%2C_Bathurst.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Chifley_House%2C_Bathurst.jpg/440px-Chifley_House%2C_Bathurst.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1621" data-file-height="1418" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Ben_Chifley%27s_House" title="Ben Chifley&#39;s House">Ben Chifley's House</a> at 10 Busby Street, Bathurst, now a heritage site and house museum</figcaption></figure>
<p>Chifley married <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Chifley" title="Elizabeth Chifley">Elizabeth McKenzie</a> (known as "Lizzie") on 6 June 1914.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay200193_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay200193-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> She was the daughter of a more senior railways employee, George McKenzie. The couple began courting in 1912, but had known each other since childhood.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay200184_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay200184-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> The McKenzies were Presbyterian, and Elizabeth did not want to convert to Chifley's Catholic faith. Due to the Catholic Church's opposition to mixed marriages, the couple chose to marry in a Presbyterian church in Glebe, Sydney. Their parents opposed the union and did not attend the ceremony, but they and their families were eventually reconciled.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay200192–94_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay200192–94-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> The McKenzies were relatively wealthy, and Chifley was seen as "marrying into money, or as much money as he could hope to marry into in the context of the relatively class-bound society of Bathurst".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay200185_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay200185-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After their marriage, Chifley's father-in-law gave the couple a house on Busby Street, Bathurst, which they would occupy for the rest of their respective lives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay200194_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay200194-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> It is now listed on the <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales_State_Heritage_Register" title="New South Wales State Heritage Register">New South Wales State Heritage Register</a> as "<a href="/wiki/Ben_Chifley%27s_House" title="Ben Chifley&#39;s House">Ben Chifley's House</a>", and has operated as a <a href="/wiki/House_museum" class="mw-redirect" title="House museum">house museum</a> since 1973. Chifley and his wife had no children. She suffered a "serious health problem", probably a miscarriage, in about 1915,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001104_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001104-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> and later developed chronic back pain that restricted her mobility. The couple lived mostly separate lives, initially because of her husband's work on the railways and later because of his political career.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001155_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001155-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> She rarely travelled outside Bathurst and never lived in Canberra, even while her husband was prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001415–417_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001415–417-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> She usually visited the city for only special occasions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001239,_439–440_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001239,_439–440-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Her health prevented her from campaigning for her husband, and she was known to have little interest in politics. Nonetheless, the couple "seemingly enjoyed a close and caring relationship throughout his life".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001157_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001157-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> She survived her husband by 11 years, dying in 1962.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001532_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001532-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to his biographer <a href="/wiki/David_Day_(historian)" title="David Day (historian)">David Day</a>, Chifley engaged in a long-running extramarital affair with his private secretary Phyllis Donnelly.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001296–297_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001296–297-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Day believed that their relationship began shortly after Chifley was elected in parliament in 1928,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001239_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001239-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> and continued more or less uninterrupted until his death in 1951; she was present in his room at the <a href="/wiki/Hotel_Kurrajong" title="Hotel Kurrajong">Hotel Kurrajong</a> when he suffered his final heart attack.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001526_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001526-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> She stayed at the same hotel, and they were known to spend their free time with each other while in Canberra.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001239–240,_404_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001239–240,_404-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> She also accompanied him on many of his travels. According to Frank Slavin, Chifley's campaign manager at the 1940 election, his wife was aware of the relationship and tolerated it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001297_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001297-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Day also speculated that Chifley may have had a similar relationship with Phyllis's older sister Nell.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001212_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001212-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> He assisted her financially in the 1930s, including buying her a house in Bathurst.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001350_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001350-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Day based his conclusions on interviews conducted with the Donnelly family and other Bathurst residents who had known Chifley. His claims have been disputed by members of the Chifley family,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDay2001212_88-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay2001212-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> and some reviewers of his book felt there was insufficient evidence to conclude that Chifley's relationship with either of the Donnelly sisters was sexual in nature.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Elizabeth_Chifley.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Elizabeth_Chifley.jpg/170px-Elizabeth_Chifley.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="193" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Elizabeth_Chifley.jpg/255px-Elizabeth_Chifley.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Elizabeth_Chifley.jpg/340px-Elizabeth_Chifley.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="680" /></a><figcaption>Mrs <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Chifley" title="Elizabeth Chifley">Elizabeth Chifley</a>, wife of Ben Chifley</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1987 the <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales" title="New South Wales">New South Wales</a> <a href="/wiki/Government_of_New_South_Wales" title="Government of New South Wales">Labor government</a> decided to name the planned new university in Sydney's western suburbs Chifley University. Controversy broke out when, in 1989, a new Liberal government renamed it the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Western_Sydney" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Western Sydney">University of Western Sydney</a>. According to a debate on the topic, held after the Labor Party had regained government, the decision to rename Chifley University reflected a desire to attach the name of <a href="/wiki/Western_Sydney" class="mw-redirect" title="Western Sydney">Western Sydney</a> to institutions of lasting significance, and that idea ultimately received the support of <a href="/wiki/Bob_Carr" title="Bob Carr">Bob Carr</a>, later the <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_New_South_Wales" title="Premier of New South Wales">Premier of New South Wales</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Honours">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ben_Chifley_bust.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Ben_Chifley_bust.jpg/170px-Ben_Chifley_bust.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Ben_Chifley_bust.jpg/255px-Ben_Chifley_bust.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Ben_Chifley_bust.jpg/340px-Ben_Chifley_bust.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="1600" /></a><figcaption>Bust of Prime Minister of Australia from 1945-49  Ben Chifley by sculptor Ken Palmer located in the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister%27s_Avenue" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister&#39;s Avenue">Prime Minister's Avenue</a> in the Ballarat Botanical Gardens</figcaption></figure>
<p>Places and institutions that have been named after Chifley include:
</p>
<ul><li>the suburb of <a href="/wiki/Chifley,_Australian_Capital_Territory" title="Chifley, Australian Capital Territory">Chifley</a> in <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a></li>
<li>the suburb of <a href="/wiki/Chifley,_New_South_Wales" title="Chifley, New South Wales">Chifley</a> in Sydney</li>
<li>the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Chifley" title="Division of Chifley">Division of Chifley</a>, a federal electorate</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ben_Chifley%27s_House" title="Ben Chifley&#39;s House">his former house in Bathurst</a>, now the Chifley Home and Education Centre,<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> listed on the <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales_State_Heritage_Register" title="New South Wales State Heritage Register">NSW State Heritage Register</a></li>
<li>Chifley Library, the main library of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_University" title="Australian National University">Australian National University</a>, Canberra</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chifley_Tower" title="Chifley Tower">Chifley Tower</a> and Chifley Square in Sydney</li>
<li>Chifley Cave (formerly the Left Imperial Cave), one of the <a href="/wiki/Jenolan_Caves" title="Jenolan Caves">Jenolan Caves</a> in the Blue Mountains, New South Wales<sup id="cite_ref-cave_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cave-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>several <a href="/wiki/List_of_Government_schools_in_New_South_Wales" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Government schools in New South Wales">public</a> <a href="/wiki/Secondary_school" title="Secondary school">high schools</a> in <a href="/wiki/Western_Sydney" class="mw-redirect" title="Western Sydney">Western Sydney</a> are now known as Chifley College.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>a grouping of <a href="/wiki/Dormitory" title="Dormitory">dormitories</a> at the Bathurst campus of <a href="/wiki/Charles_Sturt_University" title="Charles Sturt University">Charles Sturt University</a> are collectively named as Chifley Halls</li>
<li>Chifley Research Centre the official think tank of the Australian Labor Party is named in honor of Ben Chifley<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ben_Chifley_Dam" title="Ben Chifley Dam">Chifley dam</a> About 17 kms (11&#160;mi) upstream of Bathurst.</li></ul>
<p>In 1975 he was honoured on a postage stamp bearing his portrait issued by <a href="/wiki/Australia_Post" title="Australia Post">Australia Post</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of the locomotives driven by Chifley, <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales_D50_class_locomotive" title="New South Wales D50 class locomotive">5112</a>, is preserved on a plinth at the eastern end of <a href="/wiki/Bathurst_railway_station,_New_South_Wales" title="Bathurst railway station, New South Wales">Bathurst railway station</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> In 1971 <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Railways" title="Commonwealth Railways">Commonwealth Railways</a> named diesel locomotive <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Railways_NJ_class" title="Commonwealth Railways NJ class">NJ1</a> that was assembled at the <a href="/wiki/Clyde_Engineering" title="Clyde Engineering">Clyde Engineering</a> factory in <a href="/wiki/Kelso,_New_South_Wales" title="Kelso, New South Wales">Kelso</a>, <i>Ben Chifley</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_popular_culture">In popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ben_Chifley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: In popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Chifley was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Bill_Hunter_(actor)" title="Bill Hunter (actor)">Bill Hunter</a> in the 1984 TV miniseries <i><a href="/wiki/The_Last_Bastion" title="The Last Bastion">The Last Bastion</a></i>, by <a href="/wiki/Ed_Devereaux" title="Ed Devereaux">Ed Devereaux</a> in the 1988 miniseries <i><a href="/wiki/True_Believers_(miniseries)" title="True Believers (miniseries)">True Believers</a></i>, and <a href="/wiki/Geoff_Morrell_(actor)" title="Geoff Morrell (actor)">Geoff Morrell</a> in the 2007 film <i><a href="/wiki/Curtin_(2007_film)" class="mw-redirect" title="Curtin (2007 film)">Curtin</a></i>.
</p>
